# CLAUDE.md — Ancient Games

## The name

**The Ancient Games** — named by EJ, 2026-09-06. Its principles come from two poles: **孫子兵法** (ancient Chinese
strategy) on one side and **game theory** on the other, with **modern military doctrine** and **complexity science**
in between.

## What this folder is for

This folder is EJ's place for designing dynamic workflows for agent work, using the knowledge kept here.

The Ancient Games is one framework for agent work: the workflow-design method (`.claude/skills/workflow-design/`)
is the part that turns an incoming task into a designed workflow, and `ancient_games/` is the part that referees
the work, with the referee's findings feeding back into the method.

The method's skill is linked into both harness roots so it works in every project —
`~/.agents/skills/workflow-design` (the user-global root the DeepSeek Harness and Codex scan) and
`~/.claude/skills/workflow-design` (Claude Code). The knowledge below is what that skill uses.

Designing a workflow means deciding, for a given task: which kinds of agents to use, what runs in sequence and what
runs in parallel, what repeats (with its limit and stop condition), who decides who works next, how agents pass
information and where they must not, and how each result is verified. Runnable workflows live in
`.claude/workflows/`.

When EJ brings a task or a question about a workflow, the job is to help design that workflow from this knowledge.
Work on the referee code (`ancient_games/`) serves that purpose; it is not the goal by itself.

## The knowledge here

- `docs/WORKFLOW_DESIGN_METHOD.md` — the method for turning a challenge into a prompt file or a workflow design:
  understand the challenge (objective, scope, six whys, category), readiness (with a check of the product's
  blueprint), algorithm first, list the unclear spots, clear pieces as loops, then the graph; sequential first;
  state-file driven. Templates in `docs/workflow-templates/`. Runnable form:
  `.claude/workflows/intake.js` (args `{challenge, runId}`); each run writes to `runs/<runId>/`.
- `docs/TASK_TYPES.md` — the default execution pattern, engine, check and sabotage per task category, seeded
  combination pipelines, and the 20% challenger rule with `docs/TASK_TYPES_LEDGER.md`. The skill's
  `scripts/design_gate.py` parses it — this file is the table's only copy (n=0 until the ledger fills).
- `docs/WORKFLOW_DESIGN_DIAGRAM.md` — the same algorithm as diagrams: the whole flow, the blueprint check, what the
  method takes from the referee code, and the mapping from method section numbers to the skill's steps.
- `docs/EXECUTOR_KINDS.md` — who can run a node (method 3.1 / 3.6): the engines (Claude subagent, Codex, Antigravity `agy`, Qwen, opencode), what each is assigned, how it is called, its read-only form and structured output, and its
  default model and effort. A node's engine choice reads this. n=0; *reported* versus *verified* marked throughout.
- `.claude/skills/workflow-design/` — the method packaged as a skill. `SKILL.md` is the packaging and the trigger:
  its frontmatter `description` is what makes an agent decide to use it, and its body is the method as eight steps.
  Its method and templates are links into `docs/`, so there is one copy of each. It is the source of truth;
  `~/.claude/skills/workflow-design` is a link to it, which makes it available in every project.
- `20260919-state.md` — what Ancient Games can and cannot decide today about graph, loop, swarm, sequential,
  parallel and communication patterns, and the proposed direction. Start here for workflow design.
- `ancient_games/` — the referee: the part of the framework that referees agent work. Fixed rules, no LLM calls, a
  journal of every decision. Five stages: Gate (how many agents), Guard (which actions need approval), Corroborate (how many independent
  sources a claim needs), Filter (which candidates survive), Prove (is the plan cleared).
- `PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` — map of the code, how a run works, open findings F1–F11.
- `SPEC.md`, `DECISIONS.md` — the referee's contract. Only EJ changes these.
- `docs/` — design documents and ablations: what was tried, what held, what is marked n=1 and not to be built on.
- `.claude/workflows/enhance-ancient-games.js` — a worked example of a designed workflow: dependencies between
  segments, bounded verify/repair loops, independent verifiers.

## Working with EJ here

- Answer the question that was asked, in plain language, before offering options or plans.
- Discuss first. Do not plan or run anything large until EJ says so.
- Tests: `env -u NO_COLOR python3 -m pytest -q`

### When EJ states a rule, a requirement or an algorithm

Asked for by EJ, 2026-10-05: *"each time when I told you something, you will eval my statement or algo, and ask me
what I missing, and we complete it."*

**Evaluate it, do not just apply it.** Run the statement through the **`requirement-check` skill** — load it
(`skill requirement-check`) or read `~/skills/requirement-check/SKILL.md` — and say which of the eight it passes and
fails. Then **ask EJ the failing ones** and write the completed version together.

The eight, by name: **source · one thing · unit · can it fail · weak words · level · basis · links.** Their
definitions live in the skill — one copy, not four.

**Two rules for me, learned the hard way on 2026-10-05:**

- **Never fill a gap with my own value.** If the unit, threshold or test is missing, it goes to EJ as an open
  question — inventing it is how rules become weird later. Recorded in the register as 11.1/11.2.
- **Never add structure that was not given.** No "halves", no numbered reasons, no ordered pairs unless the person
  said so. Quote them, state the operative sentence, stop.

- **Ask only what changes the shape.** A question the template already answers, or that has an obvious default, is a
  question I should not ask — it is "never add structure" done to *questions* instead of facts. Three of four
  questions I asked EJ on 2026-10-06 (a large-batch cap, "may the worker refuse", the list's destination) were
  invented problems; only one changed the template. State the sensible default and move on.

### Who is this for — and who fills it in?

EJ, 2026-10-05: *"have to remember we doing things for who."* **Every artifact has one maker.** A return template is
the worker's output, so every field in it is written by the worker; a verdict is the human's decision, and it does not
share the worker's form. Before adding a field, a step or a rule, name **who fills it** — if the answer is "someone
else, later", it is a different artifact, not a field on this one.

This is the "one thing" question applied to *people* instead of *facts*: a form that two different people fill at two
different times is two forms.

### Quotes, labels, and who wrote the check

EJ, 2026-10-11: *"please add these to your memory"* — after one session in which I misled him three times. All three
are **SHOULD** (a default, overridable with a stated reason): a presentation rule is method, and method does not
earn MUST (RFC 2119 §6). Run through `requirement-check`: quoting and labelling failed **weak words**, naming the
check failed **one thing** and was split in two; the trigger and the grain are EJ's answers, not mine.

- **Quote when a source's own words are the evidence, and when a list is compressed into a count.** *"Current agent
  memory splits short-term from long-term three ways"* invited EJ to read "an agent has three memory systems"; the
  report's own line — tiers with promotion, file placement, validity windows — carries the meaning. Give the
  source's line, or the count with its items in the same breath.
- **Label a conclusion as *quoted* (with its id), *my reading*, or *not measured*.** It bites on a conclusion,
  verdict, answer or summary line, never on narrative. *"The research was never the problem"* was my reading of a
  structural check, stated as a fact.
- **Name who wrote a check** before resting a conclusion on it.
- **Name what that check does not establish.** A check I wrote and then passed is not independent evidence: the
  acceptance gate's item 6 ended as a script of mine, so "6 of 6 items pass" without that sentence reads stronger
  than it is.

Basis: n=1 each, 2026-10-11 — `runs/20261011-agent-memory-graph/run-1-report.md`,
`runs/20261011-agent-memory-graph-r2/run-2-report.md`, and the session thread. What **accepted** means is defined
once, in `docs/WORKFLOW_DESIGN_METHOD.md` Appendix A.

### Evidence lives in the project, not `/tmp`

EJ, 2026-10-06. The pass-back `evidence:` path points into `runs/<run-id>/` — **SHOULD**, not MUST: a worker may
leave evidence elsewhere with a written reason, but the default is the run's folder. **Basis: n=1** — the
code-graph-tool run's optimize node wrote its "old-vs-new" proof to `/tmp`, it was deleted, and the fix became
unverifiable. When evidence needs a persistent home, **suggest `runs/<run-id>/` and get the user's agreement** — do
not pick a path silently. Scratch in `/tmp` is fine; it is the *refer-back* artifact that must not stay there.

  relevant parts are K6, Rd1–Rd3, W1, W4, W6).
- The evidence it cites, the finding ids `C*`, `X*`, `O*`, in:

## Your task

Find what is wrong or missing in the proposal. For each problem:
- **Where:** the step, reason, must-not or design change, by its number.
- **What:** one or two sentences.
- **Failure case:** concrete inputs or a concrete sequence of events, and the wrong result it produces.
- **Severity:** *breaks* (the algorithm gives a wrong index or a wrong pass/fail), *gap* (a case it does not cover),
  or *minor* (wording, clarity).

Look in particular for:
1. A sequence of git operations (branch, merge, rebase, cherry-pick, a clone without the hooks, `--no-verify`) that
   leaves a wrong `INDEX.md` committed while the check passes.
2. An entry file the parser reads wrongly: header format, quotes, a claim without `**Why:**`, unusual file names.
3. A citation that does not say what the proposal says it says. Open the cited finding id and compare.
4. A requirement in `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` that the proposal breaks or quietly changes.
5. A part the proposal does not need: the owner's rule is that memory is simple, and a mechanism that is not needed
   is removed.

Do not write a competing algorithm. If a problem has an obvious fix, give it in one line.
If you find nothing wrong under a heading, say so. Do not invent problems to fill the list.
Read only; do not change any file in the repo.

name: requirement-check
description: Evaluate a rule, requirement, threshold or algorithm against requirement-quality criteria before applying or writing it down, and ask the person which criteria it fails. # Requirement check

A general standard (RFC 2119 / 8174), installed globally — not part of the Ancient Games framework. The
worked failure in `meaningful-names` cites the Ancient Games as one example.

A short gate between **hearing a rule** and **acting on it**. It exists because rules get written down that nobody
can trace, that count two different things at once, or that nothing can ever fail — and the cost lands months later,
when nobody can say whether the rule still means anything.

Run it when someone states a rule, and run it on yourself before writing a normative line into any document.

## The eight questions

Ask them of **one statement**. Say which it passes and which it fails, then **ask the person about the failures** —
do not fill the gaps yourself.

1. **Source.** Who said this, and where? Can you point to the exact line? *"Mine"* is a valid answer. Unlabelled is
   not.
2. **One thing.** An "and", an "or", a "/", or a second number means it is **two** statements. Split them, and give
   each its own source.
3. **Unit.** Every number says what it counts. Attempts or hours? Roles, or processes running at once? Bytes or
   files?
4. **Can it fail.** Name one concrete input that would make it fail. `TBD`, `—`, `0` and empty must be among the
   failing inputs. If you cannot name one, it is not checkable — it is a wish.
5. **Weak words.** *like, about, appropriate, as needed, logical, fast, enough, reasonable, only has to, TBD.* Replace
   each with a value, **or mark the value open**. Never invent the number.
6. **Level.** Is it **MUST** (a gate that stops work), **SHOULD** (a default that may be overridden with a stated
   reason), or **guidance**? A safety bound earns MUST. **A method does not** — see the RFC note below.
7. **Basis.** Measured, quoted from a source, or a guess? A guess is fine **if it is labelled** — `n=0`, with when it
   will be looked at again.
8. **Links.** Does it claim a relation to another rule — *"half of"*, *"pairs with"*, *"the same as"*, *"the runtime
   part"*? The person probably did not say that. **A link is its own claim and needs its own source.**

## Two rules for the agent

- **Never fill a gap with your own value.** A missing unit, threshold or test goes back as a question. Inventing it
  is exactly how a rule becomes unattributable later, and the invention is indistinguishable from the original by
  the time anyone notices.
- **Never add structure that was not given.** No halves, no numbered reasons, no ordered pairs, no named phases,
  unless the person said so. Quote them, state the operative sentence, stop. If a relationship between two rules is
  wanted, that is a separate claim with its own evidence.

## Two verified facts, worth quoting

- **RFC 2119 §6, "Guidance in the use of these Imperatives":** imperatives *"must be used with care and sparingly…
  they MUST only be used where it is actually required… they must not be used to try to impose a particular method."*
  Most design rules are method. Method does not earn MUST.
- **RFC 8174 (updates 2119):** the key words *"have the meanings specified herein only when they are in all
  capitals."* Lowercase *must* and *should* carry no defined requirement meaning. If nothing marks which lines are
  requirements, a reader cannot tell rules from explanation.

## How to answer

Evaluate first, in a table or a short list — **pass / FAIL per question**. Then ask the failing ones as questions,
one line each, with the options if you can see them. Two or three real questions beat eight formalities.

Do not report a rule as complete when you chose its missing value. Say which values you left open.

## A worked failure

> *"At most 5 subagent roles per run and at most 3 running at once."*

Four of the eight fail: **Source** — nobody could name one; **One thing** — a count of roles *and* a count of
concurrent processes; **Unit** — two different units, one word ("subagent") used for both; **Level** — a method
preference written as a cap. The sentence would not parse, and it took four days to find out why. The checklist
catches it in thirty seconds.

The same checklist, run on *"No single call runs longer than the ceiling: 8 hours"*, passes all eight — because it
names who set it, counts one thing, uses a unit, and some input (a 40-hour plan) makes it fail.

## Limits

The quality characteristics behind these questions (necessary, appropriate, unambiguous, complete, singular,
feasible, verifiable, correct, conforming) come from requirements-engineering sources — IEEE 830, ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148
and the INCOSE *Guide to Writing Requirements* — which were **not read** when this skill was written: they are
paywalled or blocked, and the list is recalled rather than verified. The two RFC points above **were** read, and the
quotes are checked. Treat the rest as a good working list, not as a citation.

Origin: `docs/research/20261005-requirement-statements/FINDINGS.md` in the Ancient Games project, 2026-10-05.
---
name: workflow-design
description: "Designing a workflow for an incoming task or challenge — or deciding that none is needed — before any agents are dispatched: understand the challenge (objective, scope, six whys, category and its default pattern), check readiness (including the product blueprint: vision, requirements with acceptance criteria, domain, architecture, data, UI, non-functional), write the algorithm first, list every unclear spot, size the pieces, run clear pieces as loops with objective checks, then wire the rest as a graph whose nodes each carry tools, context, a contract, evidence and state; sequential and state-file driven first, parallel decided last. Use whenever someone asks to design, plan, create, improve or review a workflow, an agent pipeline, an orchestration or a multi-agent / subagent setup; asks how many agents a task needs, whether to parallelise or loop, or how agents should pass information; brings a task too big for one prompt; or when you are about to write a Workflow script or dispatch several subagents without a written design. NOT for: a task of one to three obvious steps (just do it), the Workflow script API, or running an already designed workflow."

# Workflow design

This is the method half of the **Ancient Games** framework (see `CLAUDE.md`): the method designs a workflow, the
referee (`ancient_games/`) clears the plan, the knowledge (`docs/`) is shared.

The job: turn a challenge into **a prompt file** (small task) or **a workflow design** (bigger task). You are
designing, not executing. The full method with its reasons is `references/method.md`; the templates are in
`assets/templates/`. Read the method once before your first design in a session.

Why this exists: agents left alone pick a pattern first ("let's fan out five subagents") and discover later that
they lacked information, that a loop had no way to close, or that two parallel workers collided. This method makes
the cheap discoveries first.

## First: understand the challenge (method 3.0)

From the challenge text alone — no tools, no agents — state the **objective**, what is **in scope** and what is
**out of scope**, answer the **six whys** (stop when an answer no longer changes the design), and end with a
**provisional category** from `references/task-types.md`. The table of whys, what each feeds, and what an
unanswerable one becomes: method 3.0.

## Then: is it tiny?

If it is tiny, do not design a workflow: do the task, or fill in `assets/templates/prompt-file.md`. The test reads the
restatement, not the raw text — method §2. A process that costs more than the task is a failure of the method, not a
success. (Trial 1, n=1: a one-line README fix went through the full process and cost about 309k tokens, partly
because checks and handover were counted as steps. Count only the steps the challenge itself needs.)

## The steps

Work through these in order. Each step's output is a file, because files are the one memory every tool and every
agent can read; a subagent starts with nothing but its prompt. Each step is a summary; the rule itself is in the
method section named beside it.

1. **Readiness** (method 3.1). Prove each tool by running it (`scripts/readiness.py` runs the standard inventory and
   marks each line verified/reported/unknown), list the skills that already cover part of the work, and check the
   information. Anything missing becomes a preparation piece at the front. Template: `readiness.md`. A node run by
   Codex or `agy`: `references/executor-kinds.md` first — the canary, read-only versus write, the bundled-runtime
   trap, CLI first and provider second, and the COO's contract. Every node names its role, engine, exact model and
   effort (never a default).
   **Blueprint check** (method 3.1): if the task changes a product, the kind of task decides which sections of
   `docs/blueprint/` it needs, and each needed section's status (settled, draft, incomplete, missing) decides the
   flow. A piece that builds waits for settled sections — blueprint questions are not answered by silence (method
   3.1). Why: without a fixed target every review finds more to do, and the run never ends.
2. **Write the algorithm** (method 3.2). Steps that each name their check; a step you cannot write clearly, or
   whose check you cannot name, is an **unclear spot**. Then **label the pieces** by category from
   `references/task-types.md`. **A label is not a node**: a subagent hand-off must buy an objective check, a
   different kind, or context room.
3. **List every unclear spot before resolving any** (method 3.3): its kind (information · decision · unknown), what
   it blocks, what it depends on. Questions go to the person as one batch, each with a provisional assumption.
4. **Size** (method 3.4). Up to 3 steps with nothing unclear except decisions that have a default → a prompt file;
   otherwise a workflow design, **default-first** from `references/task-types.md`. Never hand an engine one huge
   prompt: hours-long work splits into six or more logical pieces. Judge risk separately from size.
5. **Clear pieces run as loops** (method 3.5) while the unclear spots are being resolved — only when loop-ready: the
   five conditions are in method 3.5. A loop closes only on an objective check; escalation is a fresh node on a
   stronger tier, never a switch inside the session.
6. **The rest is a knowledge graph** (method 3.6). Nodes, edges, a result verified before anything that depends on
   it starts, joins where contradictions are a stop. The design's edges schedule; role agents do not decide who
   works next. The design and the plan are compared by `scripts/dispatch.py validate PLAN --design DESIGN`.
7. **The workflow is a checked sequence** (method 3.7): sequential, state-file-driven steps; parallel only where the
   five conditions of method 3.7 hold, and never without naming the unit. Running a node — executors are tools, two
   timers, no polling, pass-back, the brief's template, example and standard, the researcher and explorer roles — is
   method 3.8; `scripts/dispatch.py run PLAN` enforces it in code (`--dry-run` first; `docs/DISPATCHER_DESIGN.md`).
8. **The run ends at the acceptance criteria** (method 3.6). A piece that builds names the blueprint sections it
   depends on and the criteria it covers; its stop is the three-part stop of method 3.6. Anything else found goes to
   `docs/blueprint/backlog.md`, never into the current run. The product's memory files: method §4.

## Every node carries five things

| | What to write |
|---|---|
| **Tools** | what the agent uses, each proved working |
| **Context** | exactly what it is given, and what is withheld (a verifier never sees the worker's reasoning) |
| **Contract** | the intent, the observable stop condition (for a piece that builds: its acceptance criteria pass, the baseline still passes, "must not change" holds), what it returns and in what shape, what it may and must not change |
| **Evidence** | the proof that comes back with the result (a command and its output, or file:line) and the file it is saved in |
| **State** | what it reads from the state file before starting and what it writes back |

An edge is then concrete: the earlier node's returns and evidence become the later node's context.

## State file

The state lives in a file (`assets/templates/state.md`): each agent reads it, does one step, writes the new state
back. One writer at a time. It holds progress and results, never reasoning — notes go in the worker's own output
file. This is what lets another agent, another tool, or tomorrow's session continue the run.

## Before calling a design finished

Check it shows all three — **predictability**, **debuggability**, **quality control** — and say so in the design.
What each needs, and the step that defines it: method §5.


**One more thing, and it is not a quality: the engine split.**
For build work, run `scripts/workload.py --plan <plan> --write` before the run starts. It gives **opencode priority** (EJ, 2026-10-04) by **module**, crossing each module's code and tests so the tests are written by the engine that did not write the code. The default is the *smallest* lead that counts as priority — which at five modules is exactly 60/40, costing one module its independent tests; demanding 60% on a larger plan costs far more, so `--share` is there when the bigger lead is wanted. Never split dev and tests independently: that gives half the modules their code *and* their tests from one engine. `check()` refuses a plan where opencode does not lead, that self-tests more than its lead costs, or whose verifier shares its node's engine. `docs/EXECUTOR_KINDS.md` has the rule and the arithmetic.

## Writing a rule down

Before adding or changing any rule in these documents, run it through the **`requirement-check` skill**
(source, one thing, unit, can it fail, weak words, level, basis, links). One copy of the checklist, in that skill.
**Never fill a missing unit or threshold with your own value; leave it open and ask.**

## Output

- Small task → `assets/templates/prompt-file.md`, filled in.
- Bigger task → `assets/templates/workflow-design.md`, filled in, with `state.md` and `readiness.md` beside it.
- A blueprint piece writes into the product's `docs/blueprint/`, following `assets/templates/blueprint.md`.
- Put a run's files in one folder (in the Ancient Games repo: `runs/<YYYYMMDD-slug>/`).
- Give the person the questions batch and the cost estimate before anything is run. Designing is cheap; running
  agents is not, so a run needs their go-ahead.

## Running the method as a script

`~/dev/ancient-games/.claude/workflows/intake.js` walks a challenge through steps 1–4 with one agent per step and
fixed yes/no checks in script code (args `{challenge, runId}`). It is a multi-agent run — about 300k tokens in its
one trial — so use it only when the person asks for it. Its offline test: `node tests/workflows/intake_harness.mjs`.

## Related skills

`challenge-mediation` types a challenge and rates the cost of being wrong — useful input to the risk judgment in
step 4. `task-decomposition-strategies` helps with splitting; where it leans parallel, this method's sequential-first
rule wins. `agent-loop` enforces a test loop for step 5. `chronos-ledger` tracks state across sessions.
`requirement-check` evaluates a rule before you write it; `meaningful-names` checks a name does one job.

Everything here comes from one working session and one trial (n=1); most rules have had no run at all (n=0, dates
in `docs/METHOD_CHANGELOG.md`). Treat the numbers — the 3-step rule, the cost figures — as working values to be
tested, and tell the person when a real task contradicts them.
---
name: coding-discipline
description: Baseline engineering discipline for any hands-on task in a repository. Use when writing, editing, refactoring, debugging or reviewing code, when running builds, tests or linters, when adding a dependency, or when preparing a git commit.
---

# Coding discipline

Baseline rules for working in a repository. They apply regardless of language,
framework or project.

## Before changing anything

- Read the actual file first. Never write or edit from an assumption about its contents.
- Read a neighbouring example of the same kind of thing — a sibling module, an existing
  test, an existing endpoint — and match it. The repository's own conventions outrank
  your preferences on naming, formatting, typing, file layout and error style.
- Confirm a dependency is already in use in the project, via its manifest, existing
  imports or neighbouring code, before reaching for it. Do not add a new dependency to
  solve something the existing ones already cover.

## Keep the change minimal

- Do the task that was asked. Not the adjacent task, not the cleanup you noticed on the
  way past, not the refactor that would leave this nicer than you found it.
- No speculative abstraction. Three similar lines beat a helper with one caller.
- No error handling, fallbacks or validation for conditions that cannot occur. Validate
  at boundaries only: user input, external APIs, data arriving from outside the process.
- Comments explain *why*, and only where the code cannot carry it. No comment that
  narrates what the next line does, and never a comment addressed to the reader about
  the changes you are making.
- Existing code you did not expect to find is someone's in-progress work. Leave it alone
  unless the task requires otherwise, and stop to ask if it conflicts with what you were
  asked to do.

## Verify instead of assuming

- Find the project's real check commands from its README, Makefile or package manifest.
  Never assume `npm test`, `pytest` or `make build` is correct for this repo.
- Run them after the change. A change you did not run is a change you did not finish.
- Report what actually happened. If a check failed, show the failure. If you could not
  run a check, say so and say why. Never describe unverified work as working, and never
  skip, suppress or weaken a failing check to produce a clean result.

## Git

- Before committing, run `git status`, `git diff HEAD` and `git log -n 3` — to separate
  your change from what was already in the working tree, and to match the repository's
  commit message style.
- Stage only the paths that belong to this change. No `git add -A` while unrelated
  modifications are present.
- Draft the commit message yourself, weighted toward why rather than what.
- Never push, force-push, amend a published commit, delete a branch, or rewrite history
  unless explicitly asked to in this conversation. Prior approval does not carry over to
  a later commit.
- Do not revert work that is not yours. Revert your own change only if it caused a
  failure or you are asked to.
- For history questions, `git log` and `git blame` are authoritative. Run the command
  rather than reasoning from memory of the codebase.

## When blocked or unsure

- Ask one focused question when only the user can decide, and keep it to a single round.
- Otherwise choose the safe default, do the work, and state which default you chose so
  it can be corrected.
- Investigate before working around an obstacle: read the error, check your assumption,
  address the root cause. Do not bypass a guard, a lock or a failing check just to make
  the obstacle go away.
- Clean up scratch files you created. Leave the working tree containing only the change
  you were asked for.

/bin/bash -lc 'cat runs/20261012-index-md-algorithm/proposals/claude.md' in /home/smoke01/dev/ancient-games

# INDEX.md — proposal (claude)

Read in full: `docs/memory-system/SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md`, `lit-review/claude.md` (C1–C58), `lit-review/codex.md`
(X1–X35), `lit-review/opencode.md` (O1–O43). The agy review does not exist (dropped by EJ, `state.md` log). I did not
read the other engines' stage-2 proposals. Nothing in the repo was changed.

**In one sentence:** one standard-library Python script builds `INDEX.md` from `entries/` as a pure function of the
entry files and writes it to a committed file. The same script, with `--check`, rebuilds it from a git snapshot and
compares the bytes. Whoever writes an entry runs the script, and a committed git hook runs the check on every commit or
merge that touches `project-memory/`. A conflict in `INDEX.md` is never merged by hand: it is regenerated. An index over
the size limit is never cut: the check fails, and EJ decides.

**Assumptions** (*my reading*, not stated in the design):
- The entry header is front matter between two `---` lines, written as `key: value`.
- The script lives outside `project-memory/`, so Rd1.1's layout does not change. I write `tools/memory_index.py`
  because this repo has a `tools/` folder and no `scripts/`. Any fixed path works.

## The algorithm

**The parts:**
- One script, `tools/memory_index.py`. It needs Python 3 and its standard library, nothing else.
- Two committed hook files, `.githooks/pre-commit` and `.githooks/pre-merge-commit`, with the same content.
- Two instructions in `AGENTS.md`.

There is no merge driver, no CI job (this repo has none), no session-start regeneration and no agent-specific hook.

**Building the index** (shared by both modes):

1. **Read the entry set.** Take every file directly in `project-memory/entries/` whose name matches
   `^[DFLROH]-[^/]+\.md$`, and nothing else: no subfolders, no dotfiles, not `INDEX.md`. Where the files come from
   depends on the mode:
   - **Write mode** reads the working tree.
   - **Check mode** reads git objects. With no argument it reads the staging area (`git ls-files -s`, then
     `git show :<path>`). With `--check <rev>` it reads that commit (`git ls-tree`, then `git show <rev>:<path>`).

   Decode each file as UTF-8 and turn CRLF into LF.
2. **Sort** the file names with Python's default `str` ordering, which compares code points. Never use locale
   collation or the order a directory listing returns (C2, C14, O6). Because file names start with the kind prefix, the
   index comes out grouped by kind: D, F, H, L, O, R.
3. **Parse each file.**
   - The header is the text between the first line `---` and the next `---`. From it, read only the top-level `id:`,
     `kind:` and `status:`, stripping whitespace and any surrounding quotes.
   - The **claim** is the body from after the closing `---` down to, but not including, the first line that starts
     with `**Why:**`. Drop blank lines, collapse every run of whitespace (newlines included) to one space, and strip
     the ends. W1.3 says the body opens with the one- or two-sentence claim, and this turns that claim into the one line
     K6.1 asks for. Nothing is shortened.
4. **Validate before writing anything.** Collect every problem and report all of them. The checks are:
   - the header is present;
   - `id`, `kind` and `status` are present;
   - `kind` is one of decision, fact, lesson, runbook, observation, hypothesis;
   - `status` is one of active, superseded, retired;
   - a `**Why:**` line exists and the claim is not empty;
   - no two entry files share an `id`, whatever their status.

   If any check fails, print one `<file>: <problem>` line per problem, write nothing and exit 1. These are only the
   fields the index needs. The full W1 lint is a separate check (X33, O43).
5. **Select** the entries whose `status` is `active`. Superseded and retired entries get no line, which is K6's
   "Fails if".
6. **Render** these exact bytes: UTF-8, LF line endings, one newline at the end of the file.
   ```
   # Project memory index

   <!-- GENERATED by tools/memory_index.py from project-memory/entries/. Do not edit by hand. Regenerate: python3 tools/memory_index.py -->

   - [<id>](entries/<file name>) <kind>: <claim>
   ```
   There is one `- …` line per active entry, in the order from step 2. With no active entries, the list is the single
   line `(no active entries)`. The file has no date, timestamp, count or "generated at" line (C15, O7, X8). The marker
   comment names the command that rebuilds the file (C20, O2, O39).
7. **Measure the size.** Count the lines and UTF-8 bytes of the rendered text. Compare them with `MAX_LINES` and
   `MAX_BYTES`, two constants at the top of the script whose comment cites Rd3.1 and `CACHES.md` § Example numbers by
   case. Every run prints `INDEX.md: <lines>/<MAX_LINES> lines, <bytes>/<MAX_BYTES> bytes`, so the growth is in view
   long before the limit is reached. The size is compared, not judged: there is no warning threshold.

**Write mode:** `python3 tools/memory_index.py`

8. Run steps 1–7 on the working tree.
   - If the rendered text is byte-identical to the current `project-memory/INDEX.md`, leave the file alone (C3).
   - Otherwise write a temporary file in the same folder and rename it over `INDEX.md`.
   - **If the index is over the limit, still write the whole file.** It is the true index, and K6.1 wants one line per
     active entry. Then print `over the start-file limit (Rd3.1): commits that touch project-memory/ will fail until EJ
     decides`, and exit 1.
   - Exit 0 otherwise.

   The script writes `project-memory/INDEX.md` and nothing else. It never changes an entry.

**Check mode:** `python3 tools/memory_index.py --check [<rev>]`

9. Run steps 1–7 on the git snapshot: the staging area, or `<rev>`. Compare the rendered text, byte for byte, with
   `project-memory/INDEX.md` in the same snapshot.
   - Exit 0 only if three things hold: the entries are valid, the bytes are equal, and the size is within the limit.
   - Otherwise exit 1 and print what failed. If the bytes differ, also print a unified diff (`difflib`) and the fix,
     `python3 tools/memory_index.py && git add project-memory/INDEX.md` (C54, C55, O38–O40, X31).

   Check mode reads only git objects, so it never touches the working tree and works the same in any worktree.

**When it runs, and what runs it:**

10. **The writer, right after creating or changing any entry file**, runs write mode. "The writer" is any agent or
    person, and "any entry file" means every kind and every status change, not only decisions and facts (see Design
    changes). The entry and `INDEX.md` go into **the same commit**.
    - This is `AGENTS.md` instruction 1: *"After you create or change a file in `project-memory/entries/`, run
      `python3 tools/memory_index.py` and commit `project-memory/INDEX.md` with it."*
    - It is the only trigger that every agent and every person gets, web sessions included, because it needs nothing
      installed.
11. **The git hooks, at every commit and merge that touches memory.** Each hook file does two things:
    - It runs `git diff --cached --name-only -- project-memory/`, and exits 0 if the output is empty. This keeps code
      commits from being blocked by memory.
    - Otherwise it runs `python3 tools/memory_index.py --check` (the staging area) and returns its exit code.

    The two hooks cover different commits. git's own manual (read locally, git 2.43):
    - `pre-merge-commit` runs on a merge that git completes without conflicts. A clean text merge is therefore checked,
      not trusted.
    - `pre-commit` runs on the `git commit` that concludes a conflicted merge. The manual says of a merge that stops on
      conflicts that `pre-merge-commit` "will not be executed, but the pre-commit hook will, if it is enabled".

    The hooks are switched on once per clone with `git config core.hooksPath .githooks`; worktrees share that setting.
    That command is `AGENTS.md` instruction 2.

    The check runs on the staged snapshot rather than regenerating and staging, because unstaged entries would
    otherwise leak into the commit (X15). A clone without the hooks, or a commit made with `--no-verify`, skips the
    check. The next hooked commit that touches memory checks the whole snapshot and fails until the index is fixed.
12. **Anyone, at any time:** `python3 tools/memory_index.py --check HEAD` is K6's "Fails if", runnable as written.

**Git and parallel branches:**

13. `INDEX.md` is committed (Rd1.2) and merges as normal text.
    - Each line comes from exactly one file, and the lines are sorted by file name. So entries added on different
      branches usually land on different lines and merge cleanly. *My reading*, not measured.
    - When `INDEX.md` conflicts, **nobody edits it**. First resolve the conflicting entry files (who does that is W6's
      open question). Then run write mode, `git add project-memory/INDEX.md` and commit. The `pre-commit` check
      confirms the result (X19, C38, C39).

**When the index is too big:**

14. Nothing is truncated, dropped, ranked, split or moved into an "Optional" section. The full file is written (step 8)
    and the check fails (step 9), so memory commits stop until EJ decides, for example to:
    - supersede, retire or merge entries;
    - change `MAX_LINES` / `MAX_BYTES` after measurement;
    - change K6.1 / Rd2.1 to allow a second-level index.

    The overflow mechanism is chosen when it is needed, not built in advance.

## Three supporting reasons

1. **This is the shape that ended the shared-file conflict elsewhere, and committing it is the part Rd1.2 forces.**
   - GitLab and CPython stopped writing to one shared file by hand and moved to one file per entry, with the combined
     file derived from them (C31, X17). The design already has one file per entry (W6.1).
   - A clone must be able to read the index with no generator run, which makes it a committed file (O25, C25, C34),
     not one built at read or release time (C32, O21, X20).
   - Sorting by file name puts parallel additions on different lines (C2). A conflict that still happens is resolved by
     regenerating, which FKF requires in its normative text (X19) and which mdsmith and npm automate (C38, C39).

   (findings: C31, X17, O25, C25, C34, C32, O21, X20, C2, X19, C38, C39)
2. **A deterministic generator makes K6's check exact, and one script both writes and checks.**
   - "Regenerating changes it" is a real test only if the output is a pure function of the entry files (O5). The known
     ways to break that are locale collation, directory-listing order and timestamps (C2, C9, C14, O6, C15, O7, X8),
     and steps 2 and 6 remove all three.
   - Kubernetes and Cog check generated files exactly this way: regenerate, then compare (C54, O38, C55, X31). doctoc
     shows that the writer and the checker can be one command (O40), so there is no second implementation to drift.
   - Reading git objects instead of the working tree is the same isolation Kubernetes gets from a clean worktree
     (C54), without creating one.

   (findings: O5, C2, C9, C14, O6, C15, O7, X8, C54, O38, C55, X31, O40)
3. **Failing loud on size, with no overflow mechanism yet, keeps K6.1 true and builds only what is needed.**
   - The sources disagree on overflow. Qwen Code drops lines (C46), Claude Code errors (C45, X25), mdsmith refuses
     (C52), Repomix splits (C53), and others nest or rank (O35, O36, X27, C47).
   - Every answer that keeps the file small either breaks K6.1's "one line per active entry" or changes what Rd2.1
     loads (X26, codex's "did not find" on overflow).
   - Losing lines silently is the worst outcome: past the limit, content is dropped on the next load (C45), and a long
     file gets ignored without notice (O31).
   - Headroom is large. `CACHES.md` puts the byte limit at about 107–150 lines of index (*quoted*, its table row for
     Claude Code). Karpathy reports a single index working at "~100 sources" (C51).
   - Under the owner's rule, a mechanism that is not needed is not built. The check makes the moment it becomes needed
     visible.

   (findings: C46, C45, X25, C52, C53, O35, O36, X27, C47, X26, O31, C51)

## Six must-not-dos

1. **Do not merge `INDEX.md` with `merge=union`, and do not hand-merge it.** `union` silently re-adds a line that one
   side removed, so a superseded entry comes back as active, which is exactly K6's "Fails if". It also leaves added lines
   "in random order". (finding: C36; also C35, O24, X21)
2. **Do not let the output depend on the machine.** That rules out locale collation, directory-listing order, and any
   date, timestamp or "generated at" line. Byte-identical output across machines is what stops two collaborators
   rewriting the file back and forth, and what keeps the check from raising false alarms. (finding: C2; also C14, C15,
   O6, O7, X8)
3. **Do not truncate, drop or demote lines to fit the size limit.** A cut index hides active entries from every session
   without a sign, and K6.1 requires one line per active entry. (finding: C45; also C46, O31)
4. **Do not skip a malformed entry, and do not emit a line with a hole in it.** A generator that falls back silently
   (Understory, X4) reproduces a bad input faithfully, so a freshness check alone passes it. The script fails and names
   the file. (finding: X33; also X4, O43)
5. **Do not give entries ids by "the next free number".** Two branches will both take the same number, and the clash
   shows only after the merge. Ids must be unique without coordination; blurb, reno and Beads use a datetime, a nonce or
   a hash for this. Step 4 fails on a duplicate id. (finding: C40; also C16)
6. **Do not deliver `INDEX.md` at session start through a Claude Code `SessionStart` hook's stdout.** That output is
   capped at 10,000 characters, with no setting to raise it. Past the cap only a 2,000-character preview reaches the
   agent, which is far below Rd3.1's example limit, and the agent is not told to read the rest. Load it the way Rd2.1
   says: an `AGENTS.md` instruction, or a `CLAUDE.md` import (C28). (finding: C44)

## Design changes

All of these are *proposed*; EJ decides.

1. **K6, its Open line.** Close it: the tool is `tools/memory_index.py` (the path is EJ's choice). What runs it is the
   writer (instruction 1), the committed `pre-commit` / `pre-merge-commit` hooks (instruction 2), and anyone's
   `--check HEAD`.
2. **K6.2.** Change "regenerated right after any decision or fact is written" to: *"regenerated right after any entry
   is created or changed, and committed in the same commit as that entry."* Two reasons:
   - A lesson, or a status change to `superseded`, also changes the index, and K6's "Fails if" applies to every
     regeneration.
   - A separate commit opens a window in which a branch carries the entry but not its line.
3. **K6.3 (new).** *"`INDEX.md` is never merged by hand. On a merge conflict, resolve the entries first, then
   regenerate it."* This settles W6's open question for `INDEX.md` only. It stays open for entry files.
4. **Rd3.1.** Three changes:
   - "the build checks it" becomes *"the index script checks it at every commit that touches `project-memory/`"*. There
     is no build in this repo.
   - Add: *"Over the limit, nothing is cut; memory commits fail until EJ decides."*
   - The example "about 24 KB and 200 lines" needs **one exact byte number** for `MAX_BYTES`. That is an open question
     for EJ, not a value I fill in.
5. **W1.1.** Add: *"An `id` is unique without coordination between branches; it is never the next free number."* The
   form (datetime, hash, nonce) is EJ's choice.

There are no changes to Rd1.1, W1.2 or W1.3. The script sits outside `project-memory/`, and the claim is read from the
body as it is now, with no new header field.

## Where I disagree with the other reviews

- **opencode O17, O41:** they put K6.2 on a Claude Code `PostToolUse` hook. I disagree, because that covers only Claude
  and not Codex, Qwen, opencode or people (opencode says so itself in its gaps). The `AGENTS.md` instruction plus a git
  hook covers everyone who writes.
- **opencode O16:** regenerating on `post-checkout` writes a tracked file on every branch switch and dirties the tree. I
  do not use it.
- **opencode O23, O26:** `merge=binary` and `git rerere` are not needed. With lines sorted by file name, most merges are
  clean, and the hooks check the ones that are clean but wrong. A conflict is resolved by regenerating, so there is
  nothing for rerere to remember.
- **opencode O20, O42:** they leave "fail versus push-fix in CI" open. I choose fail, at commit time: K6's "Fails if"
  is a failure, and this repo has no CI to push from.
- **opencode O30, O33:** they say "the strictest limit is Claude's 200 lines". Claude's 200 lines / 25 KB is documented
  for `MEMORY.md` only (C45, X26). The real limit depends on the load path (C44, C45, C48), and `CACHES.md` says bytes
  bind before lines. The script checks both.
- **opencode O1, O35, O37:** they suggest injecting into a region of a hand-written file, ranking, or an "Optional"
  tail. I do not adopt any of them: K6.1 makes `INDEX.md` a whole generated file with one line per active entry.
- **codex X3, X5:** the summary there comes from a frontmatter `description` field. I take the claim from the body
  instead, because W1.3 already holds it and a second copy in the header could drift from the body.
- **my own C4, C34, C47:** none of these fits this design.
  - C4: Qwen's "(also: …)" deduplication breaks one line per entry.
  - C34: fast-forward-only sync does not fit agents that work on branches and merge.
  - C47: a tree index changes Rd2.1.

# Source-of-Truth Memory Design

> **Status: accepted by EJ on 2026-10-12** (EJ: *"yes I accept the source of truth design"*), with its vision and
> its Write / Keep / Read requirements. Vision: `vision-statements/source-of-truth-memory-vision-statements.md`.
> First written 2026-10-11 by `opencode` from
> `runs/20261011-project-memory-engines/RECOMMENDATION.md`; revised with EJ on 2026-10-12.
> Sources, citations and review history: `runs/20261011-source-of-truth-design/` (the full annotated version is
> `DESIGN-detailed-2026-10-12.md`) and `runs/20261012-memory-update-timing/notes.md`.
>
> **Revised 2026-10-12:** the drift-check script and the background subagent are removed (EJ: *"ok remove both and
> update the doc"*). A decision is started when it is written and complete when its time, result and test results are
> added; whoever reads a decision or fact checks it against the code before acting on it.
>
> **Where this differs from the recommendation — EJ's decisions:** no owners and no human approval gate (anyone may
> change any entry, and every change is logged); decisions and facts come before the code; a *fact* means a constraint
> the code must respect, not something discovered afterwards; no drift-check script and no background subagent.
>
> **Not proven:** the timing split (what is written immediately and what later) is design judgement, not measured.
> Where the recommendation's engines disagreed (session-start reads, one file per writer, SQLite, how often to write),
> this design follows the majority.

## Why we need this memory

The context window is cleared at the start of every session, so anything learned is lost unless it is written down.
Project memory is plain markdown files kept in the repo with the code. Every agent and every person reads the same
files, a fresh clone has all of it, and every change shows up as a git diff. Reading it needs no private agent store,
database or outside service.

## Who it is for

Every agent and every person working on this project. There are no owners and no approval gate: anyone may change any
entry, and every change is logged. EJ decides the questions listed as not decided.

Two other kinds of memory are outside this design, and both live outside the repo: memory about one person across
projects (user memory), and memory
belonging to one agent name (agent memory, kept in the home folder at `~/.claude/agent-memory/<name>/`, the way Claude
Code does it).

## What it is for

It holds what the code cannot tell you, as typed entries:

- **Requirements, written before the code.** A **decision** is a choice the code must follow, with the reason. A
  **fact** is a truth or outside constraint the code must respect.
- **What was learned, written after.** A **lesson** comes from a failure or a human correction. An **observation**
  records what was seen. A **hypothesis** is a guess, labelled as one.
- A **runbook** says how to run or fix something; where possible it points at a script.

The order of work is: change the decision or fact first, then the code. Work that has to go the other way is a matter
for the project's workflow, not for memory. A decision about this project is a project entry, whoever made it, EJ
included.

A decision is **started** when it is written: we decided to build something, and why. It is **complete** when the
time, the result and the test results are added to it (EJ, 2026-10-12).

## What will be put in

**An entry** is one markdown file. Its header holds: `id`, `kind`, `status`, `author`, the date it was recorded,
`covers` (the code paths it is about), its source or evidence, links (`depends_on`, `supersedes`, `superseded_by`), a
`changed:` log, and, on a decision once its work is complete, `done:` (the time, the result and the test results). Its
body is the claim in one or two sentences, then **Why:**, then **How to apply:**.

**Changing entries.** Anyone may change any entry, and `AGENTS.md` too. Each change adds one line to the entry's
`changed:` log: the time, and who made it — the model ID for an agent, the name for a person. Files that are not entries
rely on git history. Each new entry gets its own file, so agents never append to one shared file. Two writers must not
edit the same file at the same time: each agent works on its own branch or worktree, and git shows a merge conflict when
two edits
touch the same lines. Edits to different lines of one file merge without warning even if they contradict each other,
so a clean merge does not mean the file is consistent.

**When to write.**

| When | What |
|---|---|
| Immediately | Decisions and facts, before the code — and `INDEX.md` is regenerated right after, so the next session sees them. A human correction. The state of unfinished work before a handoff, an interruption or a context reduction. |
| When the work a decision governs is complete | The decision's `done:` line: the time, the result and the test results. |
| At the end of a task | Other lessons, observations and hypotheses; merging duplicates; runbooks. |

**Keeping it current.** Entries may go out of date while work goes on; that is accepted. Nothing watches for it: there
is no drift-check script and no background subagent. Whoever reads a decision or fact checks it against the code before
acting on it. When they no longer match, the reader does not change the entry: it reports to EJ which entry, what the
entry says, and what the code does instead, and EJ decides whether the entry is updated or the code is fixed.

**Still open:** which tool generates `INDEX.md` and what runs it (K6); who resolves a git merge conflict (W6). Whether a
changed decision is edited in place or superseded is proposed in K4.3.

## What will not be put in

- Anything that can be read directly from the code, the tests or the commit log (W3.1). The memory itself lives in git.
- Credentials or sensitive data.
- Guesses not labelled as a hypothesis.
- Raw transcripts, or another agent's private state.
- Work described as "verified" when it is only implemented.

An entry is never deleted: it is marked `superseded` or `retired` and linked to what replaced it. Age alone never
expires an entry. Anything other agents need must not stay only in one agent's private memory; it belongs here.

## Where it is put

At the repo root:

```
AGENTS.md            standing instructions for every agent (CLAUDE.md only imports it)
project-memory/
  INDEX.md           generated, one line per active entry; never edited by hand
  entries/           one file per entry: D- decision, F- fact, L- lesson, R- runbook
  work/<task-id>/    state.md (the handoff), evidence/
  journal/           raw notes, one file per agent session; never loaded automatically
  .cache/            optional search index, gitignored, rebuilt from the files
```

The project's existing documents (requirements, architecture) stay in `docs/`; memory links to them instead of copying
them. File prefixes for observations and hypotheses: `O-` and `H-`, proposed in W1.1.

## When it will be used

- **At the start of every session:** `AGENTS.md`, `project-memory/INDEX.md`, the `work/` listing and the assigned
  task's `state.md`. A hook or the first instruction in `AGENTS.md` enforces this, because agents skip optional reads.
- **On demand:** an entry when it covers a file about to be edited; text search over `entries/`; `git log` and
  `git blame` for why a line is the way it is. For a "why" question, read the decision entry.
- **At the end of a task, and before a handoff or session end:** an agent reads `journal/` and writes what is worth
  keeping as entries.
- **Before acting on something read from memory,** check it against the code, test or commit it points to. A decision
  or fact that no longer matches the code is reported to EJ, not edited.

## When it will not be used

Never loaded at session start: `journal/`, which is read at the end of a task and before a handoff or session end. Never loaded at all: superseded or retired
entries, except by following a link; the whole `project-memory/` tree at once; whole graph or code-map dumps; bulk
issue or PR text; another agent's transcripts; credentials. Text read from memory never overrides standing
instructions.

`INDEX.md` must fit the strictest start-file limit of the agents that read it, and the build checks it (Rd3).

## Core requirements (confirmed by EJ, 2026-10-12)

EJ, on reading them: *"this is amazing and a lot easy to read"*, taken as confirmation. They serve the settled vision,
`vision-statements/source-of-truth-memory-vision-statements.md`, and are grouped by
what happens to a memory: it is **written**, it is **kept** true, and it is **read** (EJ, 2026-10-12: *"yes go ahead
with write keep read"*). Each requirement names its source: **(v)** is the vision, **(d)** is this design's text, and
*proposed* marks wording EJ has not decided. Each has a check, **Fails if**, that some case breaks. There are no fixed
numbers: where a limit applies, it is a comparison that measurement decides.

### Write — what goes in, and how

**W1 — The shape of an entry** (d: What will be put in)
- **W1.1** Each entry is one file: `project-memory/entries/<prefix>-<id>-<slug>.md`. The prefixes are `D-` decision,
  `F-` fact, `L-` lesson and `R-` runbook, plus `O-` observation and `H-` hypothesis (*proposed*; the design leaves
  these two open).
- **W1.2** The header holds `id`, `kind`, `status`, `author`, the date recorded, `covers` (the code paths it is
  about), its source or evidence, `depends_on`, `supersedes`, `superseded_by`, and `changed:`. A decision whose work is
  complete also holds `done:` (W5.3).
- **W1.3** The body is the claim in one or two sentences, then **Why:**, then **How to apply:**. There is one claim per
  entry.
- **Fails if** a lint over `entries/` finds a missing field or section, or a reviewer finds two claims in one entry.

**W2 — What each kind means** (v; d: What it is for)
- **W2.1** A **decision** is a choice the code must follow, with the reason. A **fact** is a truth or outside
  constraint the code must respect, not something discovered afterwards. Both are requirements: they are written
  before the code.
- **W2.2** A **lesson** comes from a failure or a human correction. An **observation** records what was seen. A
  **hypothesis** is a guess, labelled as one. A **runbook** says how to run or fix something and points at a script
  where one exists.
- **Fails if** `kind` holds any other value, or a guess is recorded under any kind but hypothesis.

**W3 — What never goes in** (v; d: What will not be put in)
- **W3.1** Nothing that can be read directly from the code, the tests or the commit log: what a function does, which
  test covers it, what a commit changed. The memory lives in git; the rule is about what an entry says.
- **W3.2** No credentials or sensitive data.
- **W3.3** No raw transcripts, and nothing from another agent's private state.
- **W3.4** No work described as "verified" when it is only implemented.
- **W3.5** No user memory (one person, across projects) and no agent memory (one agent name). Both live in the home
  folder.
- **W3.6** No copy of the project's existing documents (requirements, architecture in `docs/`): an entry links to them.
- **Fails if** a secret scanner finds a credential (W3.2). For W3.1 and W3.3–W3.6, a reviewer finds such content.
  Those are judged checks.

**W4 — Who writes, and the change log** (v: no owners, every change logged)
- **W4.1** Anyone, agent or person, may create or change any entry, and `AGENTS.md` too. There is no owner and no
  approval: `status` is only `active`, `superseded` or `retired`.
- **W4.2** Every change to an entry adds one line to its `changed:` log: the time, and who made it (the model ID for an
  agent, the name for a person). Earlier lines are never edited.
- **W4.3** Files that are not entries (`state.md`, `journal/`, `AGENTS.md`) rely on git history.
- **Fails if** git shows a change to an entry with no matching `changed:` line, or a `status` outside the three.

**W5 — When to write** (d: When to write)
- **W5.1** Written immediately: a decision or fact, before the code it governs; a human correction; and the state of
  unfinished work, before a handoff, an interruption or a context reduction.
- **W5.2** Written at the end of a task: other lessons, observations, hypotheses, and runbooks.
- **W5.3** A decision is started when it is written. When the work it governs is complete, the agent or person who did
  the work adds `done:` to it: the time, the result and the test results (EJ, 2026-10-12).
- **Fails if** a commit changes code under a new decision with no entry in that commit or an earlier one. For a human
  correction, it fails if the session ends without one (judged). For W5.3, it fails if a task's `state.md` says its
  work is complete and the decision it carried out has no `done:` line.

**W6 — Writers do not collide** (d: Changing entries)
- **W6.1** Each new entry gets its own file. Agents never append to one shared file; `journal/` has one file per agent
  session.
- **W6.2** Each agent works on its own branch or worktree, so two edits to the same lines meet as a merge conflict, not
  a silent overwrite.
- **Open:** who resolves a merge conflict.
- **Fails if** an edit to an entry is lost without a conflict.

### Keep — memory stays true, and nothing is lost

**K1 — The decision or fact first, then the code** (v; d: What it is for)
- **K1.1** To change behaviour that a decision or fact governs, change the entry first, then the code. Work that has to
  go the other way is a matter for the project's workflow, not for memory.
- **Fails if** git shows a commit changing the behaviour a decision or fact governs, with no change to that entry in the
  same commit or an earlier one (judged).

*K2 (the drift-check script) and K3 (the background subagent) were removed by EJ on 2026-10-12. Their numbers are not
reused, because other text cites K4–K6. A mismatch between a decision and the code is now caught when the decision is
read (Rd5).*

**K4 — Never deleted, never expired by age** (v: nothing lost; d: What will not be put in)
- **K4.1** An entry is never deleted. It is marked `superseded` or `retired`, links to what replaced it
  (`superseded_by`), and keeps its old body.
- **K4.2** Age alone never expires an entry. An entry is found out of date only when someone reads it and checks it
  against the code (Rd5).
- **K4.3** *Proposed:* a decision refined without changing its meaning is edited in place, with a `changed:` line; a
  decision replaced by a different one gets a new entry that supersedes it. The design leaves this open.
- **Fails if** git shows an entry file deleted, or a `superseded` or `retired` entry has no link.

**K5 — The journal becomes entries** (v: nothing lost between sessions)
- **K5.1** Task and project memory keep `journal/`: raw notes, one file per agent session, appended to and never
  edited.
- **K5.2** At the end of a task, and before a handoff or session end, an agent reads the journal written since the
  last read, writes what is worth keeping as entries, and logs that read in the task's `state.md`. A session cut off
  before it can read loses nothing: the next read covers it (EJ, 2026-10-12: no separate milestones, the start and the
  end already cover them).
- **Fails if** a task's `state.md` shows it complete or handed off with no journal-read log line.

**K6 — `INDEX.md` stays in step** (d: Where it is put; When to write)
- **K6.1** `INDEX.md` is generated from `entries/`: one line per active entry, showing its id, kind and one-line
  claim. It is never edited by hand.
- **K6.2** It is regenerated right after any decision or fact is written, so the next session sees it.
- **Open:** which tool generates it and what runs it (a build detail).
- **Fails if** regenerating `INDEX.md` changes it, meaning it was out of step, or it lists a superseded entry as
  active.

### Read — the right memory reaches the agent at the right time

**Rd1 — Where it lives, and readable from a clone alone** (v: plain files; never a database or service)
- **Rd1.1** `AGENTS.md` sits at the repo root (`CLAUDE.md` only imports it). `project-memory/` holds `INDEX.md`,
  `entries/`, `work/<task-id>/` (with `state.md` and `evidence/`), `journal/`, and `.cache/`, which is
  optional and gitignored.
- **Rd1.2** With no network, no cache and no private agent store, a fresh clone can read `INDEX.md` and every entry.
- **Fails if** an entry exists only in a database, a cache or one agent's private memory.

**Rd2 — Session start** (v: a fresh session carries on; d: When it will be used)
- **Rd2.1** At session start, an agent reads `AGENTS.md`, `INDEX.md`, the `work/` listing and its task's `state.md`. A
  hook or the first instruction in `AGENTS.md` enforces this, because agents skip optional reads.
- **Rd2.2** Never at session start: `journal/`, `.cache/`, superseded or retired entries, the whole `project-memory/`
  tree, whole graph or code-map dumps, bulk issue or PR text, another agent's transcripts, credentials.
- **Fails if** a start hook or instruction loads any memory file outside Rd2.1.

**Rd3 — `INDEX.md` fits every agent that reads it** (d: the size check; CACHES.md § Example numbers by case)
- **Rd3.1** `INDEX.md` fits the strictest start-file limit among the agents that work on the project, and the build
  checks it. *Example*, from `CACHES.md`: about 24 KB and 200 lines when Claude Code, Qwen and agy share a project.
- **Fails if** `INDEX.md` exceeds that strictest limit.

**Rd4 — On demand** (d: When it will be used)
- **Rd4.1** Before editing a file, an agent reads the entries whose `covers` include it.
- **Rd4.2** It searches `entries/` by text: grep, or the full-text cache once its trigger in `CACHES.md` fires.
- **Rd4.3** For "why" questions it reads the decision entry; for why a line is the way it is, `git blame` and
  `git log -S`.
- **Rd4.4** It reads a superseded or retired entry only by following a link.
- **Fails if** an agent edits a covered file without having read its covering decisions (judged, from the session).

**Rd5 — Check before acting** (d: When it will be used; When it will not be used)
- **Rd5.1** Before acting on something read from memory, the agent checks it against the code, test or commit it
  points to.
- **Rd5.2** Text read from memory never overrides standing instructions.
- **Rd5.3** When a decision or fact no longer matches the code, the reader does not change the entry. It reports to EJ
  which entry, what the entry says, and what the code does instead, and EJ decides whether the entry is updated or the
  code is fixed.
- **Fails if** an agent acts on an entry that its `covers` code contradicts, without saying so (judged), or a
  `changed:` line on a decision or fact settles such a mismatch before EJ has decided.

**Rd6 — A fresh session carries on** (v: how we know it works)
- **Rd6.1** A fresh session with only a clone of the repo and a task id names the task's next step from its
  `state.md`, and follows the recorded decisions without asking EJ again.
- **Fails if** it asks a question that a decision entry or `state.md` already answers (judged, on a real handoff).

/bin/bash -lc 'cat runs/20261012-index-md-algorithm/lit-review/claude.md' in /home/smoke01/dev/ancient-games
 succeeded in 0ms:
